\displaystyle\geq 2-2|\langle\phi|\psi\rangle|,

which can be rearranged to

\displaystyle|\langle\phi|\psi\rangle|\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}.

By the identity for trace distance of pure states,

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|,|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-|\langle\psi|\phi\rangle|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-\left(1-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon^{2}-\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{4}}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon.

This completes the proof.
∎

4.2
Quantum Cryptographic Primitives

Definition 4.2

(One-way Puzzles)
.

A one-way puzzle is a pair of sampling and verification algorithms
(\mathsf{Samp},\mathsf{Ver})

with the following syntax.

•

\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})\rightarrow(s,k)
, is a QPT algorithm that outputs a pair of classical strings
(s,k)
.
We refer to
s
as the puzzle and
k
as its key. Without loss of generality we may assume that
k\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}(s,k)\rightarrow\top
or
\bot
,
is a Boolean function that maps every pair of classical strings
(k,s)
to either
\top
or
\bot
.

These satisfy the following properties.

•

Correctness.
Outputs of the sampler pass verification with overwhelming probability, i.e.,

\Prr_{(s,k)\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[\mathsf{Ver}(s,k)=\top]=1-\mathsf{negl}(n)

•

Security.

Given
s
, it is (quantum) computationally infeasible to find
k
satisfying
\mathsf{Ver}(s,k)=\top
, i.e., for every quantum polynomial-sized adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
and every quantum advice state
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,

\Prr_{(s,k)\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[\mathsf{Ver}(s,\mathcal{A}(|\tau\rangle,s))=\top]=\mathsf{negl}(n)

Definition 4.3

(
\varepsilon
-Distributional One-way Puzzles)
.

For
\varepsilon:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, a
\varepsilon
-distributional one-way puzzle is defined by a quantum polynomial-time generator
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
that outputs a pair of classical strings
(s,k)
such that
for every quantum polynomial-time adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
, every (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, for large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathsf{SD}\left(\{s,k\}\,\{s,{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,s)\}\right)\geq\varepsilon(n)

where
(s,k)\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
.

We will sometimes simply refer to distributional one-way puzzles. This is taken to mean
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzles for some non-zero polynomial
p
.

The following theorem shows that distributional one-way puzzles can be amplified to (standard) one-way puzzles.

Theorem 4.3

(Theorem 33 from
[
CGG24
]
, rephrased)
.

If there exists a polynomial
p(\cdot)
for which
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzles exist, then one-way puzzles exist.

Definition 4.4

(
\varepsilon
-Pseudo-deterministic Hard Distributions)
.

An algorithm
{\mathcal{B}}
is
\varepsilon
-pseudo-deterministic for
\varepsilon:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
if

\Prr_{r}[\exists y\text{ s.t. }\mathrm{Pr}\left[{\mathcal{B}}(r)\neq y\right]\leq\mathsf{negl}(n)]>1-\varepsilon(n)

where
r
is a uniformly random string.

A family of efficiently sampleable distributions
D=\{D_{n}\}
is
\varepsilon
-pseudo-deterministic hard if for all quantum polynomial-time
\varepsilon
-pseudodeterministic adversaries
{\mathcal{A}}
that take (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, for all
large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}

\mathsf{SD}({\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle;r),D_{n})>\varepsilon(n)

where
r
is a uniformly random string.

5
Hardness of Approximating Probabilities implies One-way Puzzles

In this section we define notions of distributions with hard to approximate probabilities and prove their equivalence with one-way puzzles.

5.1
Definitions

We begin by presenting a hardness assumption that has been extensively studied in the literature on sampling based quantum advantage
[
AA11
,
BMS16
,
FM17
,
BIS+18
,
BFNV19
,
KMM21
,
Kro22
,
Mov23
,
HE23
]
). Here, the adversary is given uniformly sampled
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
together with a quantum circuit
C
on
n
qubits (with possibly
m=m(n)
ancillas). The adversary’s task is to estimate
\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
, i.e. the probability that the output state of
C|0^{n+m}\rangle
results in outcome
x
when measured in the standard basis.
The adversary is required to output a low
relative error
approximation to
\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
, i.e. for some polynomial
p(\cdot)
and for sufficiently many
x
, the adversary must output a value
y
such that

\left(1+\frac{1}{p(n)}\right)\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\leq y\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{p(n)}\right)\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]

The outputs of circuit
C
are required to satisfy an additional property called anticoncentration, which says that a noticeable fraction of strings
x
have probability mass in
C
above a particular threshold of
1/(p(n)\cdot 2^{n})
.

Definition 5.1

(Uniform Approximation Hardness)
.

A family of (uniform) efficiently sampleable distributions
{\mathcal{C}}=\{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
over quantum circuits
C
that output classical strings in
\{0,1\}^{n}
has uniform approximation hardness if it has the following properties. There exist polynomials
p(\cdot)
and
\gamma(\cdot)
such that:

1.

Anticoncentration.
For all large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{n}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\end{subarray}}[\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\geq 1/(p(n)\cdot 2^{n})]\geq 1/\gamma(n)

2.

Approximate Hardness of Sampling.
For any oracle
{\mathcal{O}}
satisfying that for all large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{n}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\end{subarray}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{O}}(C,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]}{p(n)}\right]\geq 1/\gamma(n)-\frac{1}{p(n)}

we have that
\mathsf{P}^{\#\mathsf{P}}\subseteq\mathsf{BPP}^{{\mathcal{O}}}
.
Here,
\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
denotes that probability the
C
outputs
x
.

While Definition
5.1
captures the hardness conjectures proposed in the quantum advantage literature, it is slightly inconvenient for cryptographic applications.
There are a few reasons for this: the biggest one is that only adversaries that succeed for
all
large enough
n
will help solve
\#\mathsf{P}
. The standard adversarial model for cryptography allows for adversaries that succeed on infinitely many
n
.
Second, hardness is defined for strings sampled uniformly, whereas for our applications, it will be more suitable to have hardness defined for strings sampled from the output of the circuit itself.
Finally, we only require hardness against all polynomial-sized quantum machines, whereas the definition above requires
\#\mathsf{P}
hardness of the approximation task.

We will therefore define and build from Definition
5.1
(along with the assumption that
\mathsf{P}^{\#\mathsf{P}}\not\subseteq\mathsf{ioBQP}/\mathsf{qpoly}
) a related but more “crypto-friendly” primitive that will simplify the implication to puzzles (Theorem
5.1
).

Definition 5.2

(Native Approximation Hardness)
.

A family of (uniform) efficiently sampleable distributions
{\mathcal{D}}=\{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
over classical strings
has native approximation hardness if there exists a polynomial
p
such that for all QPT
{\mathcal{A}}=\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, every (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\Prr_{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]}{p(n)}\right]\leq 1-\frac{1}{p(n)}

Next we will show (Theorem
5.2
) that the existence of distribution families satisfying Definition
5.2
implies the existence of one-way puzzles (Definition
4.2
). Finally we will show the reverse implication (Theorem
5.3
), namely that the existence of one-way puzzles implies the existence of distribution families satisfying Definition
5.2
.

5.2
Uniform Approximation Hardness Implies Native Approximation Hardness

Theorem 5.1
.

If
\mathsf{P^{\#P}/qpoly}\not\subseteq\mathsf{ioBQP/qpoly}
and there exists a family of distributions
{\mathcal{C}}=\{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
that satisfies Definition
5.1
, then there exists a family of distributions
{\mathcal{D}}=\{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
that satisfies Definition
5.2
.

Proof.

Let
L
be a
\mathsf{PP}
-complete language.
{\mathcal{C}}
satisfies Definition
5.1
, so there must exist exist polynomials
q
and
\gamma
along with oracle PPT
R^{(\cdot)}_{L}
such that:

1.

For all large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{n}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\end{subarray}}[\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\geq 1/(q(n)\cdot 2^{n})]\geq 1/\gamma(n)

2.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be any oracle such that for all large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{n}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\end{subarray}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{O}}(C,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]}{q(n)}\right]\geq\frac{1}{\gamma(n)}-\frac{1}{q(n)}

then for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and for all
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\Prr[R^{\mathcal{O}}_{L}(x)=L(x)]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(n)
. We call the set of all such oracles
{\mathbb{O}}
.

Let
{t_{R}}
be a polynomial such that
R^{(\cdot)}_{L}
runs in time
{t_{R}}(n)
.

Since
L
is
\mathsf{PP}
-complete,
\mathsf{BQP}^{L}/\mathsf{qpoly}=\mathsf{BQP}^{\mathsf{PP}}/\mathsf{qpoly}=\mathsf{BQP^{\#P}/qpoly}
. Let
L^{\prime}\in\mathsf{BQP}^{L}/\mathsf{qpoly}
such that
L^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{ioBQP/qpoly}
. Then there exists an oracle QPT
{\mathcal{M}}^{(\cdot)}=\{{\mathcal{M}}^{(\cdot)}_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\sigma\rangle=\{|\sigma_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, for all
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\Prr[{\mathcal{M}}^{L}(|\sigma\rangle,x)=L^{\prime}(x)]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(n)

We will drop the advice from the notation since it is always implicitly provided to
{\mathcal{M}}
. Additionally, let
{t_{\mathcal{M}}}
be a polynomial such that
|{\mathcal{M}}_{n}|\leq{t_{\mathcal{M}}}(n)
.

Our overall proof strategy will be to show that (given an adversary that estimates probabilities for a carefully constructed distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
) we can replace the oracle to
L
in
{\mathcal{M}}^{L}
with an efficient quantum algorithm (with advice). This is accomplished by using
R_{L}^{(\cdot)}
with an appropriately constructed oracle to decide
L
instead. We wish to define
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
in such a way that any adversary that estimates the probabililities of
y\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
will also allow us to estimate
\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
for
C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
and
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
. We may initially try setting
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
to be the induced distribution on
(C,x)
. The adversary will in this case provide an estimate of
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}}[C]\cdot\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
. To compute
\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
we therefore also need to compute
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}}[C]
. This is accomplished by having
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
consist of two modes, one in which the output is
(C,x)
and one in which the output is
C
alone. This allows us to compute estimates for both
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}}[C]\cdot\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
and
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}}[C]
and therefore estimate
\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
.

The above strategy is still insufficient for instantiatiating an oracle for
R^{(\cdot)}_{L}
. This is because an adversary that breaks the security of
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
may only do so for infinitely many values of
n
. The reduction
R^{(\cdot)}_{L}
, on the other hand, requires an oracle that breaks security for every large enough value of
n
. While it is tempting to think that instantiating
R^{(\cdot)}_{L}
with an infinitely-often oracle would lead to a reduction that also succeeds infinitely often, this is not necessarily the case.

R^{(\cdot)}_{L}
may query its oracle on a variety of input sizes, and only succeed if the oracle performs well on all of them. We must therefore be able to answer queries for all input sizes up to
t_{R}(m)
when running
R^{(\cdot)}_{L}
on inputs of size
m
. Since
R^{(\cdot)}_{L}
will ultimately be queried by
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}(x)
for
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
m
can be as large as
t_{\mathcal{M}}(n)
.
We therefore modify
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
in such a way that we can use an adversary breaking its security to answer queries of all sizes upto
t_{R}(t_{\mathcal{M}}(n))
.

Let
{t}
be any polynomial such that
{t}(n)>{t_{R}}({t_{\mathcal{M}}}(n))
and let
p
be any polynomial such that
p(n)>nq^{4}({t}(n))\cdot({t}(n))^{3}
.
We define
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
as follows:

•

Sample
\ell\leftarrow[{t}(n)]

•

Sample
C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}

•

Sample
x\leftarrow C

•

Sample
b_{\text{mode}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}

•

If
b_{\text{mode}}=0
:

–

Output
(b_{\text{mode}},C,0^{\ell})

•

Else:

–

Output
(b_{\text{mode}},C,x)

We will prove that
{\mathcal{D}}
satisfies Definition
5.2
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that this is not the case. Then there exists a QPT
{\mathcal{A}}=\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for infinitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\Prr_{y\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,y)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[y]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[y]}{p(n)}\right]>1-\frac{1}{p(n)}

Fix any such adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
, any such advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle
, and any such large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We will drop the advice from the notation since it is always implicitly provided to the adversary. We will now use
{\mathcal{A}}
to build a QPT
{\mathcal{B}}
that calls
{\mathcal{A}}
internally such that for all
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, with high probability,
R^{{\mathcal{B}}}(x)=L(x)
. Specifically, we define
{\mathcal{B}}
as the algorithm that takes input
(C,x)
and returns
{\mathcal{A}}(1,C,x)/{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{|x|})
.

First we show that we can replace the oracle
{\mathcal{O}}
with
{\mathcal{B}}
for queries of size less than
t(n)
. We may view the above inequality as bounding the probability that
{\mathcal{A}}(y)
is too far from
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[y]
, i.e.

\Prr_{y\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(y)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[y]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[y]}{p(n)}\right]<\frac{1}{p(n)}

We can split this error probability into cases indexed by the values of
b_{\text{mode}}
and
\ell
. First consider when
b_{\text{mode}}=0
. For all
\ell\in[{t}(n)]

\displaystyle\Prr[b_{\text{mode}}=0]\cdot\Prr[\ell]\cdot\Prr_{C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{\ell})-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[0,C,0^{\ell}]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[0,C,0^{\ell}]}{p(n)}\right]<\frac{1}{p(n)}

which can be simplified to

\displaystyle\forall\ell\in[{t}(n)],\Prr_{C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}\left[\left|2{t}(n)\cdot{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{\ell})-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}{p(n)}\right]<\frac{2{t}(n)}{p(n)}

(1)

Similarly, consider when
b_{\text{mode}}=1
. For all
\ell\in[{t}(n)]

\displaystyle\Prr[b_{\text{mode}}=1]\cdot\Prr[\ell]\cdot{\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow C\end{subarray}}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(1,C,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[1,C,x]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[1,C,x]}{p(n)}\right]<\frac{1}{p(n)}

which can be simplified to

\displaystyle\forall\ell\in[{t}(n)],\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow C\end{subarray}}\left[\left|2{t}(n)\cdot\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(1,C,x)}{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}-\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]}{p(n)}\right]<\frac{2{t}(n)}{p(n)}

(2)

Let
\delta:=\sqrt{\frac{2{t}(n)}{p(n)}}
. We will now show for every large enough
\ell
the existence of a “good” set of
(C,x)
that is sampled with high enough probability and for which
{\mathcal{B}}(C,x)
gives a good estimate of
\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
with high probability. For all
\ell\in[t(n)]
let
{\mathbb{C}}_{\ell}
be defined as follows.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}_{\ell}:=\left\{C\text{ s.t }\Prr\left[\left|2{t}(n)\cdot{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{\ell})-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}{p(n)}\right]>\delta\right\}

Intuitively,
{\mathbb{C}}_{\ell}
is the set of
C
output by
{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}
such that with all but
\delta
probability,
2{t}(n)\cdot{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{\ell})
is a good estimate for
\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]
.
By a Markov argument on (
1
), for all
\ell\in[t(n)]

\Prr_{C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\ell}]\leq\delta

Similarly, for all
\ell\in[t(n)]
let
{\mathbb{G}}_{\ell}
be defined as follows.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{G}}_{\ell}:=\left\{(C,x)\text{ s.t }\Prr\left[\left|2{t}(n)\cdot\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(1,C,x)}{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}-\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]}{p(n)}\right]>\delta\right\}

Intuitively,
{\mathbb{G}}_{\ell}
is the set of
(C,x)
where
C
is output by
{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}
and
x
is an
\ell
-bit string such that with all but
\delta
probability,
2{t}(n)\cdot\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(1,C,x)}{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}
is a good estimate for
\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]
. By a Markov argument on (
2
), for all
\ell\in[t(n)]

\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow C\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{\ell}]\leq\delta

Claim 5.1
.

For all large enough
\ell\in[{t}(n)]
, let
{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}:=\left\{(C,x)\text{ s.t }C\notin{\mathbb{C}}_{\ell}\wedge(C,x)\notin{\mathbb{G}}_{\ell}\right\}

•

For all
(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}
,
\Prr\left[\left|{\mathcal{B}}(C,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{C}(x)\right|\leq\frac{3\mathrm{Pr}_{C}(x)}{p(n)}\right]\geq 1-2\delta

•

\mathrm{Pr}_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}]\geq 1/\gamma(\ell)-2\delta\cdot q(\ell)

Proof.

For the first part of the claim, note that by the definitions of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\ell}
, for all
(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}
, with probability atleast
(1-\delta)

\left|2{t}(n)\cdot{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{\ell})-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}{p(n)}

which can be rearranged as

\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{\ell})}{(1-1/p(n))}<\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}{2{t}(n)}<\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{\ell})}{(1+1/p(n))}

Additionally, by the definitions of
{\mathbb{G}}_{\ell}
, for all
(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}
, with probability atleast
(1-\delta)

\left|2{t}(n)\cdot\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(1,C,x)}{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}-\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]}{p(n)}

which can similarly be rewritten as

\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\cdot(1-1/p(n))<2{t}(n)\cdot\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(1,C,x)}{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}<\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\cdot(1+1/p(n))

Both events occur simultanaeously with probability atleast
1-2\delta
, in which case we may apply the bounds for
\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]}{2{t}(n)}
to the previous inequality.

\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\cdot(1-1/p(n))^{2}<\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(1,C,x)}{{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{\ell})}<\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\cdot(1+1/p(n))^{2}

which for large enough
n
gives

\left|\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]-\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(1,C,x)}{{\mathcal{A}}(0,C,0^{\ell})}\right|<\frac{3\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]}{p(n)}

which concludes the proof of part 1.

For the second part of the claim, first note that since
\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow C\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{\ell}]\leq\delta
and
\Prr_{C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\ell}]\leq\delta

\Prr_{C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C\in\overline{{\mathbb{B}}}_{\ell}]\leq 2\delta

where
\overline{{\mathbb{B}}}_{\ell}
represents the complement of
{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}
.
Let
{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}:=\left\{(C,x):\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]\geq 1/(q(\ell)\cdot 2^{\ell})\right\}
.
By the anticoncentration property we know that for large enough
\ell\in[{t}(n)]

\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}]\geq 1/\gamma(\ell)

We aim to bound
\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}\cap\overline{{\mathbb{B}}}_{\ell}]
as follows

\displaystyle\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}\cap\overline{{\mathbb{B}}}_{\ell}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(C,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}\cap\overline{{\mathbb{B}}}_{\ell}}\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]\cdot 1/2^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(C,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}\cap\overline{{\mathbb{B}}}_{\ell}}\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}[C]\cdot q(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}_{C}[x]

\displaystyle={q(\ell)}\cdot\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow C\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}\cap\overline{{\mathbb{B}}}_{\ell}]

\displaystyle\leq{q(\ell)}\cdot\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow C\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in\overline{{\mathbb{B}}}_{\ell}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta\cdot q(\ell)

where the first step follows from the definition of
{\mathbb{A}}
and the last step follows from Claim
5.1
. We can now bound
\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}]
as follows

\displaystyle\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}]

\displaystyle\geq\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}\cap{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}]

\displaystyle=\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}]-\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}_{\ell}\cap\overline{{\mathbb{B}}}_{\ell}]

\displaystyle\geq 1/\gamma(\ell)-2\delta\cdot q(\ell)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Claim 5.2
.

For all
m
such that
{t_{R}}(m)\leq{t}(n)
, for all
x\in\{0,1\}^{m}

\Prr[R_{L}^{{\mathcal{B}}}(x)=L(x)]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(m)-2\delta\cdot{t}(n)

Proof.

Since
R_{L}^{(\cdot)}(x)
runs in time atmost
{t_{R}}(|x|)
, the size of the longest query to made is atmost
{t_{R}}(m)\leq{t}(n)
. Additionally, the number of queries made is also atmost
{t_{R}}(m)\leq{t}(n)
. By Claim
5.1
and by noting that
p(n)>nq^{4}({t}(n))\cdot({t}(n))^{3}
and
\delta=\sqrt{\frac{2{t}(n)}{p(n)}}
, for every
\ell\leq{t}(n)
, there exists a set
{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}

•

For all
(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}
,
\Prr\left[\left|{\mathcal{B}}(C,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{C}(x)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{C}(x)}{q(t(n))}\right]\geq 1-2\delta

•

\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\leftarrow{{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}}\\
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}[(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}]\geq 1/\gamma({\ell})-1/q(t(n))

Let
{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}
be the set of oracles such that for every
\ell\leq{t}(n)
, for all
(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}
,

\left|{\mathcal{O}}(C,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{C}(x)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{C}(x)}{q(\ell)}

Since
\ell\leq t(n)
it is easy to see that
{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{O}}
.

R^{(\cdot)}_{L}(x)
succeeds with probability
1-\mathsf{negl}(m)
for every
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}
, so it succeeds with probability atleast
1-\mathsf{negl}(m)
given any random distribution over oracles in
{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}
. We will now show that with high probability,
{\mathcal{B}}
is such a distribution over oracles.

Assume WLOG that
R^{{\mathcal{B}}}_{L}(x)
queries any string atmost once during its execution. We only need to consider queries of length atmost
{t}(n)
since no query is longer than
{t}(n)
. If for every query
(C,x)\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\ell}
made to
{\mathcal{B}}
where
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, it was the case that
\left|{\mathcal{B}}(C,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{C}(x)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{C}(x)}{q(n)}
then the outputs of
{\mathcal{B}}
are distributed according to some distribution over
{\mathbb{O}}^{\prime}
. For each query this occurs independently with probability atleast
1-2\delta
and there are at most
{t}(n)
queries, so this event occurs with probability atleast
1-2\delta\cdot{t}(n)
. Therefore,
\mathrm{Pr}[R_{L}^{\mathcal{B}}(x)=L(x)]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(m)-2\delta\cdot{t}(n)
.
11
11

11

A slightly different version of Definition
5.1
requires the oracle to approximate probabilities upto arbitrary relative error
\epsilon
(given
1^{1/\epsilon}
as input) and with probability
1/\gamma({\ell})-\delta
(given
1^{1/\delta}
as input) over the randomness of the input. In this case, note that since the size of the largest query is atmost
t(n)
,
\epsilon
and
\delta
are atleast
1/t(n)
.
{\mathcal{B}}
can therefore still be used to answer such oracle queries by setting
p(n)
to be large enough to achieve relative error
1/t(n)
with probability
1/\gamma({\ell})-1/t(n)
over the randomness of the input, and Theorem
5.1
will be unaffected by the change in the definition.

∎

Noting that
p(n)>nq^{4}({t}(n))\cdot({t}(n))^{3}
and
n
is large enough, we get
2\delta\cdot{t}(n)\leq 1/3
. Therefore, by repeating in parallel and taking the majority outcome we obtain an oracle PPT
S^{(\cdot)}
where for all
m
such that
{t_{R}}(m)\leq{t}(n)
, for all
y\in\{0,1\}^{m}
,
\mathrm{Pr}[S^{\mathcal{B}}(y)=L(y)]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(m)
. Finally, we will use
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{B}}}
to decide
L^{\prime}
on strings of length
n
. For
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
M^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{B}}}_{n}(x)
can only query strings of length at most
{t_{\mathcal{M}}}(n)
. Since
{t_{R}}({t_{\mathcal{M}}}(n))\leq{t}(n)
, this means that
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{B}}
will correctly answer the queries with all but negligible probability. As a result, for all
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{M}}_{n}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{B}}}(x)=L^{\prime}(x)]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(n)
.

Finally, since
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{B}}
is a polynomial size quantum circuit with quantum advice,
{\mathcal{M}}^{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{B}}}
may also be expressed as a polynomial size quantum circuit with quantum advice that decides
L^{\prime}
for infinitely many input lengths. This contradicts the assumption that
L^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{ioBQP/qpoly}
and concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.3
Native Approximation Hardness Implies One-Way Puzzles

Theorem 5.2
.

The existence of families of distributions that satisfy Definition
5.2
implies the existence of one-way puzzles (Definition
4.2
)

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}=\{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a family of distributions that satisfies Definition
5.2
. Therefore there exists a polynomial
q
such that for all QPT
{\mathcal{A}}=\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, every (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle\Prr_{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]}{q(n)}\right]\leq 1-\frac{1}{q(n)}

We may also assume w.l.o.g. that the outputs of
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
are of
n
bits. Define
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
as follows:

•

Sample
i\leftarrow[0,n-1]

•

Sample
x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}

•

Output puzzle
x_{1\ldots i}
and key
x_{i+1}

Let
p(n)
be any polynomial such that
p(n)>n^{6}q(n)^{3}
.
We will prove that
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
is a
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzle. Since Theorem
4.3
shows that distributional one-way puzzles can be amplified to obtain (strong) one-way puzzles, this suffices to prove the theorem.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
is
not
a
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzle. Then there exists a QPT
{\mathcal{A}}=\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for infinitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathsf{SD}\left(\{x_{1\ldots i},x_{i+1}\},\{x_{1\ldots i},{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,x_{1\ldots i})\}\right)\leq 1/p(n)

where
x_{1\ldots i},x_{i+1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
.
Fix any such adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
, any such advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle
, and any such large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We will drop the advice from the notation since it is always implicitly provided to the adversary.

First we define some useful terms. For
i\in[0,n-1]
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{i}
,
z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n-i}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
:

•

Define
p_{z}:=\mathrm{Pr}_{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x_{1\ldots i}=z]
, i.e. the probability that the first
i
bits of
x
sampled from
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
match
z
.

•

Define
p_{z^{\prime}|z}:=\Prr_{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}\left[x_{i+1\ldots n}=z^{\prime}\ \middle|\ x_{1\ldots i}=z\right]
i.e. the probability that the last
n-i
bits of
x
sampled from
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
match
z^{\prime}
conditioned on the first
i
bits of
x
matching
z
.

•

Similarly define
p_{b|z}:=\Prr_{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}\left[x_{i+1}=b\ \middle|\ x_{1\ldots i}=z\right]
i.e. the probability that the
i+1
-th bit of
x
sampled from
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
is
b
conditioned on the first
i
bits of
x
matching
z
.

•

Define
\widetilde{p}_{b|z}:=\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{A}}(z)=b]

We will use the adversary to build
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
that contradicts the security of
{\mathcal{C}}
. We will first build an estimator
{\mathcal{E}}_{b}
that takes input
z
and estimates
\widetilde{p}_{b|z}
.

For
i\in[0,n-1]
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{i},b\in\{0,1\}
define the algorithm
{\mathcal{E}}_{b}(z)
as:

•

For
j=1
to
16n^{7}q(n)^{4}
:

–

X_{j}\leftarrow{\mathbb{I}}\{A(z)=b\}

•

Return
\sum_{j}X_{j}/16n^{7}q(n)^{4}

where
{\mathbb{I}}
is an indicator function. We now define the algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
that takes input
x
and computes an approximation to
p_{x}
. Define
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)
as

•

Return
\Pi_{j\in[0,n-1]}{\mathcal{E}}_{x_{j+1}}(x_{1\ldots j})

Observe that
p_{x}=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
. On a high level, to approximate
p_{x}
it therefore suffices to approximate
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
for every
j
and multiply the approximations. We cannot directly estimate
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
however
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
uses
{\mathcal{E}}_{x_{j+1}}(x_{1\ldots j})
to estimate
\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
instead. We will use the fact that
{\mathcal{A}}
distributionally inverts
\mathsf{Samp}
to argue that this is sufficient to (on average) obtain an approximation to
p_{x}
.

First, we show that
{\mathcal{E}}_{b}(z)
is a good approximation of
\widetilde{p}_{b|z}
with high probability.

Claim 5.3
.

For all
i\in[0,n-1]
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{i},b\in\{0,1\}

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{b}(z)-\widetilde{p}_{b|z}\right|\leq\frac{1}{4n^{3}q(n)^{2}}\right]\geq 1-2e^{-n}

Proof.

Follows from setting
\delta=\sqrt{n}
in the additive Chernoff bound (Theorem
4.1
).
∎

The estimate we obtain has an inverse polynomial additive error. If the value being estimated is too small, then this leads to a large relative error in the estimate. We define the set
{\mathbb{B}}
as containing all
x
such that
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
is atleast
1/n^{2}q(n)
for every index
j
. Formally

{\mathbb{B}}:=\left\{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}\text{ s.t.}\forall j\in[0,n-1],p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}\geq 1/n^{2}q(n)\right\}

Intuitively,
{\mathbb{B}}
contains strings for which the additive error induced by Claim
5.3
only leads to a small relative error. Next we show that with high probability
x
sampled from
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
is in
{\mathbb{B}}
.

Claim 5.4
.

\Prr_{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x\in{\mathbb{B}}]\geq 1-2/nq(n)

Proof.

For each index
j
we define the set
{\mathbb{S}}_{j}
as the set of strings
x
such that
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
is less than
1/n^{2}q(n)
. Intuitively, if a string
x
is not in
{\mathbb{B}}
, it must be in
{\mathbb{S}}_{j}
for some
j
. Therefore, we can prove the claim by bounding the probability of sampling a string in
{\mathbb{S}}_{j}
for every index
j
. Formally, for all
j\in[0,n-1]

{\mathbb{S}}_{j}:=\left\{x\text{ s.t. }p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}<1/n^{2}q(n)\right\}

Now, since
p_{x}=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
, the probability of sampling
x
depends on
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
for every index
j
. If
x\in{\mathbb{S}}_{j}
then
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
is small, which allows us to bound the probability of sampling such an
x
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}\left[x\in{\mathbb{S}}_{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{S}}_{j}}\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in{\mathbb{S}}_{j}\end{subarray}}p_{x_{1\ldots j}}\cdot p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j+1}}\cdot p_{x_{j+2\ldots n}|x_{1\ldots j+1}}

\displaystyle<\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in{\mathbb{S}}_{j}\end{subarray}}p_{x_{1\ldots j}}\cdot 1/n^{2}q(n)\cdot p_{x_{j+2\ldots n}|x_{1\ldots j+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{0,1\}^{n}\end{subarray}}p_{x_{1\ldots j}}\cdot 1/n^{2}q(n)\cdot p_{x_{j+2\ldots n}|x_{1\ldots j+1}}

\displaystyle=2/n^{2}q(n)

By a union bound, this implies

\mathrm{Pr}_{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}\left[\exists j\text{ s.t. }x\in{\mathbb{S}}_{j}\right]\leq 2/nq(n)

Since for all
x\notin{\mathbb{B}}
,
\exists j\text{ s.t. }x\in{\mathbb{S}}_{j}

\mathrm{Pr}_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}\left[x\in{\mathbb{B}}\right]\geq 1-2/nq(n)

which concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Next we define the set of strings
x
such that for all
j
a good estimate of
\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
is also a good estimate of
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
. Define the set
{\mathbb{D}}
as follows.

{\mathbb{D}}:=\left\{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}\text{ s.t.}\forall j\in[0,n-1],\left|\widetilde{p}_{1|x_{1\ldots j}}-p_{1|x_{1\ldots j}}\right|\leq 1/4n^{3}q(n)^{2}\right\}

We now use the fact that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a distributional inverter to show that with high probability
x
sampled from
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
is in
{\mathbb{D}}
.

Claim 5.5
.

\Prr_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}[x\in{\mathbb{D}}]\geq 1-4n^{5}q(n)^{2}/p(n)

Proof.

Recall that

\mathsf{SD}\left(\{x_{1\ldots i},x_{i+1}\},\{x_{1\ldots i},{\mathcal{A}}(x_{1\ldots i})\}\right)\leq 1/p(n)

where
(x_{1\ldots i},x_{i+1})\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
. Since
i
is chosen uniformly, we may split the statistical distance into terms for each value of
i
, i.e. for
x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[0,n-1]}\mathrm{Pr}_{i\leftarrow[0,n-1]}[i=j]\cdot\mathsf{SD}\left(\{x_{1\ldots j},x_{j+1}\},\{x_{1\ldots j},{\mathcal{A}}(x_{1\ldots j})\}\right)\leq 1/p(n)

\displaystyle\implies\forall j\in[0,n-1],\mathsf{SD}\left(\{x_{1\ldots j},x_{j+1}\},\{x_{1\ldots j},{\mathcal{A}}(x_{1\ldots j})\}\right)\leq n/p(n)

Expanding the statistical distance term, we may rewrite the expression as

\forall j,{\mathbb{E}}_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}\left[\left|\widetilde{p}_{1|x_{1\ldots j}}-p_{1|x_{1\ldots j}}\right|\right]\leq n/p(n)

By a Markov argument,

\forall j,\Prr_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}\left[\left|\widetilde{p}_{1|x_{1\ldots j}}-p_{1|x_{1\ldots j}}\right|\geq\frac{1}{4n^{3}q(n)^{2}}\right]\leq 4n^{4}q(n)^{2}/p(n)

By a union bound

\Prr_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}\left[\forall j,\left|\widetilde{p}_{1|x_{1\ldots j}}-p_{1|x_{1\ldots j}}\right|\geq\frac{1}{4n^{3}q(n)^{2}}\right]\leq 4n^{5}q(n)^{2}/p(n)

which by the definition of
{\mathbb{D}}
implies

\Prr_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}[x\in{\mathbb{D}}]\geq 1-4n^{5}q(n)^{2}/p(n)

concluding the proof of the claim.
∎

To complete the proof we will first show that for all
x\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap{\mathbb{D}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)
is a good approximation to
p_{x}
with high probability. Then we show that with high probability
x\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap{\mathbb{D}}
when
x
is sampled from
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
. To show the former, we will need the following lemma. The lemma shows that for some real values
\{a_{i},b_{i}\}_{i\in[0,n-1]}
, if
b_{i}
is a good (i.e. low relative error) estimate of
a_{i}
for all
i
then
\prod_{i}b_{i}
is a good estimate of
\prod_{i}a_{i}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For all
i\in[0,n-1]
, let
a_{i},b_{i},\delta
be values such that

•

0<a_{i}\leq 1

•

0\leq\delta<1/n

•

\frac{\left|a_{i}-b_{i}\right|}{a_{i}}\leq\delta

Then

\frac{\left|\Pi_{i}a_{i}-\Pi_{i}b_{i}\right|}{\Pi_{i}a_{i}}\leq 2n\delta

Proof.

Let
a:=\Pi_{i}a_{i}
and
b:=\Pi_{i}b_{i}
. Then

b=\Pi_{i}b_{i}=\Pi_{i}a_{i}\cdot\frac{b_{i}}{a_{i}}=a\cdot\Pi_{i}\left(1+\frac{a_{i}-b_{i}}{a_{i}}\right)

Since
\left|\frac{a_{i}-b_{i}}{a_{i}}\right|\leq\delta\leq 1/n
and
a>0

\displaystyle a\left(1-\delta\right)^{n}\leq b\leq a(1+\delta)^{n}

\displaystyle\implies a\left(1-n\delta\right)\leq b\leq a/(1-n\delta)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|1-\frac{b}{a}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max\left(n\delta,\frac{n\delta}{1-n\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2n\delta

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Now we can show that for all
x\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap{\mathbb{D}}
, with high probability
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)
is a good approximation of
p_{x}
.

Claim 5.6
.

\forall x\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap{\mathbb{D}}

\Prr\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]}{q(n)}\right]\geq 1-2ne^{-n}

Proof.

We start by noting that for all
j
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{x_{j+1}}(x_{1\ldots j})
is close
\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
with high probability. Formally, by Claim
5.3
, for all
j\in[0,n-1]

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{x_{j+1}}(x_{1\ldots j})-\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}]\right|\leq\frac{1}{4n^{3}q(n)^{2}}\right]\geq 1-2e^{-n}

By a union bound

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\forall j\in[0,n-1],\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{x_{j+1}}(x_{1\ldots j})-\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{4n^{3}q(n)^{2}}\right]\geq 1-2ne^{-n}

Since
x\in{\mathbb{D}}
,
\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
is close to
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
. Formally,
\forall j\in[0,n-1]

\left|p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{4n^{3}q(n)^{2}}

By the triangle inequality, this shows that
{\mathcal{E}}_{x_{j+1}}(x_{1\ldots j})
is close
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
with high probability.

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\forall j\in[0,n-1],\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{x_{j+1}}(x_{1\ldots j})-p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2n^{3}q(n)^{2}}\right]\geq 1-2ne^{-n}

This gives us a bound on the additive error. To obtain a bound on relative error, we note that for all
x\in{\mathbb{B}}
,
\forall j\in[0,n-1],p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}\geq 1/n^{2}q(n)
. Therefore we can divide by
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\forall j\in[0,n-1],\frac{\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{x_{j+1}}(x_{1\ldots j})-p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}\right|}{p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}}\leq\frac{1}{2nq(n)}\right]\geq 1-2ne^{-n}

Finally we use Lemma
5.1
to show that the product of good estimates for
p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
is a good estimate for
p_{x}
. For all
j\in[0,n-1]
, let
a_{j}:=p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}
and
b_{j}:={\mathcal{E}}_{x_{j+1}}(x_{1\ldots j})
. Let
\delta:=1/2nq(n)
. Note that
\Pi_{j}a_{j}=\Pi_{j}p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}=p_{x}
and
\Pi_{j}b_{j}={\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)
. Then applying Lemma
5.1
to the above

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\frac{\left|{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)-p_{x}\right|}{p_{x}}\leq 1/q(n)\right]\geq 1-2ne^{-n}

which after rearranging gives

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)-p_{x}\right|\leq p_{x}/q(n)\right]\geq 1-2ne^{-n}

concluding the proof of the claim.
∎

Finally, combining Claim
5.4
and Claim
5.5
we can show that

\Prr_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}\left[x\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap{\mathbb{D}}\right]\geq 1-4n^{5}q(n)^{2}/p(n)-2/nq(n)

By Claim
5.6

\displaystyle\Prr_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]}{q(n)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\Prr_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}[x\in{\mathbb{B}}\cap{\mathbb{D}}]\cdot\left(1-2e^{-n}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-4n^{5}q(n)^{2}/p(n)-2/nq(n)\right)\left(1-2e^{-n}\right)

Since
p(n)>n^{6}q(n)^{3}
and
n
is large enough,

\displaystyle\Prr_{x\leftarrow{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]}{p(n)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-4n^{5}q(n)^{2}/p(n)-2/nq(n)\right)\left(1-2e^{-n}\right)

\displaystyle>\left(1-4/nq(n)-2/nq(n)\right)\left(1-2e^{-n}\right)

\displaystyle>(1-1/q(n))

which contradicts Definition
5.2
, concluding the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.4
One-Way Puzzles Imply Native Approximation Hardness

Theorem 5.3
.

The existence of distibutional one-way puzzles (Definition
4.3
) implies the existence of families of distributions that satisfy Definition
5.2
.

Let
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
be a
1/q(n)
-distributional one-way puzzle for some polynomial
q
that samples
n
bit puzzles and
n
bit keys. Let
{\mathcal{D}}=\{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a family of distributions where
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
is defined as follows:

1.

(s,k)\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})

2.

i\leftarrow[0,n-1]

3.

x\leftarrow s\|k_{1\dots i}

4.

b_{\text{mode}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}

5.

If
b_{\text{mode}}=0
, then
\beta\leftarrow\{0,1\}
. If
b_{\text{mode}}=1
, then
\beta\leftarrow k_{i+1}

6.

Output
(x,\beta)

Let
p
be a polynomial such that
p(n)>5nq(n)
. We will prove that
{\mathcal{D}}
satisfies Definition
5.2
, i.e.
for all QPT
{\mathcal{A}}=\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, every (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\Prr_{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]}{p(n)}\right]\leq 1-\frac{1}{p(n)}

Note that this suffices to prove the theorem.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
{\mathcal{D}}

does not
satisfy Definition
5.2
. Therefore there exists a QPT
{\mathcal{A}}=\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for infinitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle\underset{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}{\Prr}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}[x]}}{p(n)}\right]>1-1/{p(n)}.

Fix any such adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
, any such advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle
, and any such large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We will drop the advice from the notation since it is always implicitly provided to the adversary.
We will show that we can use
{\mathcal{A}}
to contradict one-wayness of
\mathsf{Samp}
.

First we define some useful terms.
For all
j\in[0,n-1]
,
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{j}
, and
b\in\{0,1\}
we define:

•

Define
p_{s}:={\Prr_{s^{\prime},k\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}}[s=s^{\prime}]
i.e. the probability that
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
samples puzzle
s
.

•

Define
p_{b|sz}:=\Prr_{s^{\prime},k\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}\left[k_{i+1}=b\ \middle|\ s^{\prime}=s\wedge k_{1\ldots i}=z\right]
i.e. the probability that the
i+1
-th bit of key
k
sampled by
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
equals
b
conditioned on
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
sampling puzzle
s
and the first
i
bits of
k
matching
z
.

For all
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
j\in[0,n-1]
,
k\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, define
{{\mathcal{S}}}(s,k_{1\dots j}):=

1.

\widetilde{a}_{1}\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}(s\|k_{1\dots j}\|1)

2.

\widetilde{a}_{0}\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}(s\|k_{1\dots j}\|0)

3.

\pi:=\frac{3\widetilde{a}_{1}-\widetilde{a}_{0}}{2(\widetilde{a}_{1}+\widetilde{a}_{0})}

If
\pi>1
, set
\pi\leftarrow 1

If
\pi<0
, set
\pi\leftarrow 0

4.

Sample
k_{j+1}\leftarrow\text{Bern}(\pi)
, return
k_{j+1}
.

Intuitively,
{{\mathcal{S}}}(s,k_{1\dots j})
aims to sample from the distribution on
k_{j+1}
induced by sampling from
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
conditioned on
s
and
k_{1\dots j}
, i.e. to sample
k_{j+1}
with probability
p_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\ldots j}}
. Define
\widetilde{p}_{b|sz}:=\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{S}}(s,z)=b]
.

For all
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, define
{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}(s):=

1.

For
j=0
to
n-1
:

•

k_{j+1}\leftarrow{{\mathcal{S}}}(s,k_{1\dots j})
(i.e. if
j=0
,
k_{1}\leftarrow{{\mathcal{S}}}(s)
)

2.

Return
k
.

Intuitively,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(s)
aims to use the sampler
{\mathcal{S}}
to sample bit-by-bit from the distribution induced on keys
k
sampled by
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
conditioned on sampling puzzle
s
. More formally, we will prove that

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}(\{s,k\},\{s,{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}(s)\})\leq\frac{1}{q(n)},

where
(s,k)\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
.

Note that
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[(s,k)]
may be expressed in terms of the probability of sampling
s
and the probability of sampling
k_{j+1}
conditioned on having sampled
s
and
k_{1\ldots j}
.

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[(s,k)]=p_{s}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}p_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\ldots i}}

The probability distribution
\{s,{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}(s)\}_{s,k\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}
may also be expressed similarly

\Prr_{(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[s^{\prime}=s]\cdot\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(s)}[k]=p_{s}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\ldots i}}

To argue that the two distributions are close, we will define a series of hybrid distributions interpolating between them.
For all
j\in[0,n-1]
, define distribution
\widetilde{D}_{j}
on
\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
as follows. For all
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
k\in\{0,1\}^{n}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{\widetilde{D}_{j}}[s,k]:=

\displaystyle\ p_{s}\cdot p_{k_{1}|s}\cdot p_{k_{2}|sk_{1}}\cdot p_{k_{3}|sk_{1,2}}\ldots p_{k_{j}|sk_{1\ldots j-1}}\cdot\widetilde{p}_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\ldots j}}\ldots\widetilde{p}_{k_{n}|sk_{1\ldots n-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ p_{s}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\dots i}}\prod_{i=j}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}

Note that
\widetilde{D}_{0}=\{s,{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}(s)\}_{s,k\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}
and
\widetilde{D}_{n}=\{s,k\}_{s,k\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}
.
Therefore, by the triangle inequality for statistical distance

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}(\{s,k\},\{s,{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}(s)\})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SD}(\widetilde{D}_{0},\widetilde{D}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\mathsf{SD}(\widetilde{D}_{j+1},\widetilde{D}_{j})

(3)

where
(s,k)\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
. To upper bound
\mathsf{SD}(\{s,k\},\{s,{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}(s)\})
, it therefore suffices to upper bound
\mathsf{SD}(\widetilde{D}_{j+1},\widetilde{D}_{j})
. For any
j\in[0,n-1]

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}(\widetilde{D}_{j+1},\widetilde{D}_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{s,k}\left|\mathrm{Pr}_{\widetilde{D}_{j+1}}[s,k]-\mathrm{Pr}_{\widetilde{D}_{j}}[s,k]\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{s,k}p_{s}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\dots i}}\left|p_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}\right|\prod_{i=j}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\dots i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{s,k_{1\ldots j+1}}p_{s}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\dots i}}\left|p_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}\right|\cdot\sum_{k_{j+2\ldots n}}\prod_{i=j}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\dots i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{s,k_{1\ldots j+1}}p_{s}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\dots i}}\left|p_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}\right|

(4)

The value of
\left|p_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}\right|
expresses how far the output distribution of
{\mathcal{S}}(s,k_{1\dots j})
is from the distribution of
k_{j+1}
conditioned on
s,k_{1\dots j}
. In the next subclaim we show that this term is small if for all
b\in\{0,1\}
,
{\mathcal{A}}(s,k_{1\dots j},b)
is close to
\Prr_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[s\|k_{1\dots j}\|b]
with high probability.

Claim 5.7
.

For all
s\in\{0,1\}^{n},k\in\{0,1\}^{n},j\in[0,n-1]
, define
\tau_{s,k_{1\ldots j}}
as follows.

\tau_{s,k_{1\ldots j}}:=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\mathrm{Pr}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(s,k_{1\ldots j},b)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[s\|k_{1\ldots j}\|b]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[s\|k_{1\ldots j}\|b]}{p(n)}\right]

then

\left|p_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}\right|\leq 7/p(n)+\tau_{s,k_{1\ldots j}}

Proof.

First we note that since by definition
p_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}+p_{0|sk_{1\dots j}}=1
and
\widetilde{p}_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}+\widetilde{p}_{0|sk_{1\dots j}}=1

\left|p_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}\right|=\left|p_{0|sk_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{0|sk_{1\dots j}}\right|

For
b\in\{0,1\}
, let
a_{b}:=\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[s\|k_{1\dots j}\|b]
. By the construction of
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
, for
b\in\{0,1\}

a_{b}=\mathrm{Pr}[i=j]\cdot\Prr_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[s^{\prime}=s\wedge k^{\prime}_{1\dots j}=k_{1\dots j}]\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{p_{b|sk_{1\dots j}}}{2}\right)

This allows us to express
p_{b|sk_{1\dots j}}
in terms of
a_{b}
. More precisely we can say

p_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}=\frac{3a_{1}-a_{0}}{2(a_{0}+a_{1})}

Recall that
{\mathcal{S}}(s,k_{1\ldots j})
computes
\widetilde{a}_{1}
and
\widetilde{a}_{0}
, and then calculates
\pi=\frac{3\widetilde{a}_{1}-\widetilde{a}_{0}}{2\widetilde{a}_{1}+2\widetilde{a}_{0}}
. Finally it samples a bit according to Bern
(\pi)
. Therefore we can bound the distance between
p_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}
and
\widetilde{p}_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}
in the case where for all
b\in\{0,1\}
,
\widetilde{a}_{b}
is close to
a_{b}
.

SubClaim 5.1
.

If for some sampled
\widetilde{a}_{0}
and
\widetilde{a}_{1}
the following two conditions hold:

•

|\widetilde{a}_{0}-a_{0}|\leq\frac{a_{0}}{p(n)}

•

|\widetilde{a}_{1}-a_{1}|\leq\frac{a_{1}}{p(n)}

Then
|\pi-p_{1|sk_{1\ldots j}}|\leq\frac{6}{p(n)}
.

Proof.

Let
t:=a_{1}/a_{0}
and
\widetilde{t}:=\widetilde{a}_{1}/\widetilde{a}_{0}
. Since
a_{0}\geq 0
and
a_{1}\geq 0
we can bound
\widetilde{t}
using
t
as follows.
\widetilde{a}_{0}\geq(1-1/p(n))\cdot a_{0}
and
\widetilde{a}_{1}\leq(1+1/p(n))\cdot a_{1}
so for large enough
n

\displaystyle\widetilde{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+1/p(n)}{1-1/p(n)}\cdot t

\displaystyle\leq(1+3/p(n))\cdot t

Similarly,
\widetilde{a}_{0}\leq(1+1/p(n))\cdot a_{0}
and
\widetilde{a}_{1}\geq(1-1/p(n))\cdot a_{1}
so for large enough
n

\displaystyle\widetilde{t}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1-1/p(n)}{1+1/p(n)}\cdot t

\displaystyle\geq(1-3/p(n))\cdot t

Now,
p_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}=\frac{3a_{1}-a_{0}}{2(a_{0}+a_{1})}=\frac{3t-1}{2(t+1)}
and
\pi=\frac{3\widetilde{a}_{1}-\widetilde{a}_{0}}{2(\widetilde{a}_{0}+\widetilde{a}_{1})}=\frac{3\widetilde{t}-1}{2(\widetilde{t}+1)}
so

\displaystyle|p_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}-\pi|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{3t-1}{2(t+1)}-\frac{3\widetilde{t}-1}{2(\widetilde{t}+1)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{3}{2}-\frac{4}{2(t+1)}-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{4}{2(\widetilde{t}+1)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{2}{(\widetilde{t}+1)}-\frac{2}{(t+1)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{2(t-\widetilde{t})}{(\widetilde{t}+1)(t+1)}\right|

We have shown above that
|t-\widetilde{t}|\leq 3t/p(n)

\displaystyle|p_{1|sk_{1\dots j}}-\pi|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{4t/p(n))}{(\widetilde{t}+1)(t+1)}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{p(n)}\cdot\left|\frac{t}{(\widetilde{t}+1)(t+1)}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{p(n)}\cdot\left|\frac{1}{(\widetilde{t}+1)(1+1/t)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6}{p(n)}

∎

The subclaim shows that when
|\widetilde{a}_{0}-a_{0}|\leq\frac{a_{0}}{p(n)}
and
|\widetilde{a}_{1}-a_{1}|\leq\frac{a_{1}}{p(n)}
then
|\pi-p_{1|sz}|\leq\frac{6}{p(n)}
. Additionally note that
|\pi-p_{1|sz}|
cannot exceed 1. We can therefore unconditionally bound
|\pi-p_{1|sz}|
in terms of the probability of sampling such
\widetilde{a}_{0},\widetilde{a}_{1}
. Define
\tau^{\prime}
as

\tau^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Pr}\left[\left(\left|\widetilde{a}_{0}-a_{0}\right|>\frac{a_{0}}{p(n)}\right)\vee\left(\left|\widetilde{a}_{1}-a_{1}\right|>\frac{a_{1}}{p(n)}\right)\right]

We can therefore express
\left|p_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}\right|
as

\displaystyle\left|p_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{k_{j+1}|sk_{1\dots j}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq(1-\tau^{\prime})\cdot 6/p(n)+\tau^{\prime}\cdot 1

\displaystyle=6/p(n)+(1-6/p(n))\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 6/p(n)+\tau^{\prime}

The statement of the claim follows from the observation that
\tau_{s,k_{1\ldots j}}\geq\tau^{\prime}

∎

We can use the claim to rewrite (
4
) as

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}(\widetilde{D}_{j+1},\widetilde{D}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{s,k_{1\ldots j+1}}p_{s}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\dots i}}\cdot\left(6/p(n)+\tau_{s,k_{1\ldots j}}\right)

\displaystyle=3/p(n)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{s,k_{1\ldots j+1}}p_{s}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\dots i}}\cdot\tau_{s,k_{1\ldots j}}

which can be plugged into (
3
) to get

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}(\{s,k\},\{s,{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}(s)\})

\displaystyle\leq 3n/p(n)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{j,s,k_{1\ldots j+1}}p_{s}\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{k_{i+1}|sk_{1\dots i}}\cdot\tau_{s,k_{1\ldots j}}

\displaystyle=3n/p(n)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,s,k_{1\ldots j+1}}\cdot\Prr_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[s^{\prime}=s\wedge k^{\prime}_{1\dots j}=k_{1\dots j}]\cdot\tau_{s,k_{1\ldots j}}

(5)

Recall that by assumption, with high probability over
x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
,
\left|{\mathcal{A}}(x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|
is bounded, i.e.:

\displaystyle\underset{x\leftarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}{\Prr}\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}[x]}}{p(n)}\right]<1/{p(n)}

which can be expressed as the following sum

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\cdot\Prr\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}[x]}}{p(n)}\right]<1/p(n)

and by the construction of
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[s\|k_{1\dots j}\|b]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}[i=j]\cdot\Prr_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[s^{\prime}=s\wedge k^{\prime}_{1\dots j}=k_{1\dots j}]\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{p_{b|sk_{1\dots j}}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\Prr_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[s^{\prime}=s\wedge k^{\prime}_{1\dots j}=k_{1\dots j}]\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{p_{b|sk_{1\dots j}}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\Prr_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[s^{\prime}=s\wedge k^{\prime}_{1\dots j}=k_{1\dots j}]}{4n}

so the above sum can be rewritten as

\displaystyle 1/p(n)>

\displaystyle\sum_{j,x=s\|k_{1\ldots j+1}\|b}\frac{\Prr_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[s^{\prime}=s\wedge k^{\prime}_{1\dots j}=k_{1\dots j}]}{4n}\cdot\Prr\left[\left|{\mathcal{A}}(x)-\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}}[x]\right|>\frac{\mathrm{Pr}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}[x]}}{p(n)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j,s,k_{1\ldots j+1}}\frac{\Prr_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[s^{\prime}=s\wedge k^{\prime}_{1\dots j}=k_{1\dots j}]}{4n}\cdot\tau_{s,k_{1\ldots j}}

Plugging this back into (
5
) and recalling that
p(n)>5nq(n)

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}(\{s,k\},\{s,{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}(s)\})

\displaystyle\leq 3n/p(n)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot 4n/p(n)

\displaystyle=5n/p(n)

\displaystyle<1/q(n)

which contradicts the security of
\mathsf{Samp}
.

6
The Hardness of Pseudo-Deterministic Sampling implies One-Way Puzzles

In this section we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 6.1
.

If
1/q(n)
-pseudo-deterministic hard distributions (Definition
4.4
) exist for some non-zero polynomial
q
, then one-way puzzles exist.

Proof.

Let
D_{n}
be a
1/q(n)
-pseudo-deterministic hard distribution on
n
bits. We define a candidate puzzle
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
as follows:

•

Sample
i\leftarrow[0,n-1]

•

Sample
x\leftarrow D_{n}

•

Output puzzle
x_{1\ldots i}
and key
x_{i+1}

Let
p(n)
be a polynomial greater than
2nq(n)
. We prove that
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
is a
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzle. Since Theorem
4.3
shows that distributional one-way puzzles can be amplified to obtain (strong) one-way puzzles, this suffices to prove the theorem.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that
\mathsf{Samp}
is not a
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzle. Therefore, there exists a QPT
{\mathcal{A}}=\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for infinitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}\left(\{x_{1\ldots i},x_{i+1}\},\{x_{1\ldots i},{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,x_{1\ldots i})\}\right)<\frac{1}{p(n)}

(6)

where
x_{1\ldots i},x_{i+1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
.
Fix any such adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
, any such advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle
, and any such large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We will drop the advice from the notation since it is always implicitly provided to the adversary.

We first define an estimator
E
that takes input a string
z
and performs the following:

1.

For
j
in
[25n^{4}q(n)]
:

X_{j}\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}(z)

2.

Return
\sum_{j}X_{j}/25n^{4}q(n)

Intuitively,
E
outputs an estimate of the probability that
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
outputs
1
.
Define the algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z;R)
that takes input
z
and randomness
R\in[2^{n}]
and performs the following:

1.

e\leftarrow E(z)

2.

If
2^{n}\cdot e>R
then return
1
else return
0

Define the algorithm
{\mathcal{B}}(1^{n};R_{1},R_{2},\ldots R_{n})
that takes randomness
\{R_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
where
R_{i}\in[2^{n}]
and performs the following:

1.

For
j=0
to
n-1
:

x_{j+1}\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x_{1\dots j};R_{j+1})
(i.e. if
j=0
,
x_{1}\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(\epsilon)
)

2.

Return
x
.

We will now show that
{\mathcal{B}}
contradicts the security of
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Claim 6.1

(Correctness)
.

\mathsf{SD}({\mathcal{B}}(1^{n}),D_{n})<1/q(n)

Proof.

We first show that the the output distribution of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is negligibly close to that of
{\mathcal{A}}
.

SubClaim 6.1
.

For any
z\in\{0,1\}^{<n}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z)=1]-\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{A}}(z)=1]\right|\leq 1/2^{n}

Proof.

{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
uses
E
to obtain a probability estimate
e
, and then uses the random coins
R
to output
1
if
2^{n}\cdot e>R
, else output
0
. Therefore, for any estimate
e
, the probability that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
outputs
1
equals the probability that
2^{n}\cdot e>R
. Since
R
is uniformly sampled from
[2^{n}]
, this probability is
\frac{\lfloor{2^{n}\cdot e}\rfloor}{2^{n}}
which is at most
1/2^{n}
far from
e
. For a fixed
z
, comparing the probability of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z)
returning
1
and the expectation of
E(z)
we therefore obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z)=1]-{\mathbb{E}}[E(z)]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{e}\mathrm{Pr}[E(z)=e]\cdot\frac{\lfloor{2^{n}\cdot e}\rfloor}{2^{n}}-\sum_{e}\mathrm{Pr}[E(z)=e]\cdot e\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{e}\mathrm{Pr}[E(z)=e]\cdot\left(\frac{\lfloor{2^{n}\cdot e}\rfloor}{2^{n}}-e\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e}\mathrm{Pr}[E(z)=e]\cdot\left|\left(\frac{\lfloor{2^{n}\cdot e}\rfloor}{2^{n}}-e\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e}\mathrm{Pr}[E(z)=e]\cdot\frac{1}{2^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}

Additionally, since
E(z)
simply returns the average of random variables
X_{i}
, each of which has expected value
\Prr_{\mathcal{A}}[{\mathcal{A}}(z)=1]
, the expected value of
E(z)
is also
\Prr_{\mathcal{A}}[{\mathcal{A}}(z)=1]
. Therefore

\left|\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z)=1]-\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{A}}(z)=1]\right|\leq 1/2^{n}

∎

A straightforward consequence of SubClaim
6.1
is that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is a valid adversary for
\mathsf{Samp}
. Formally,

SubClaim 6.2
.

\mathsf{SD}\left(\{x_{1\ldots i},x_{i+1}\},\{x_{1\ldots i},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x_{1\ldots i})\}\right)<\frac{1}{p(n)}+1/2^{n}.

where
i\leftarrow[0,n-1]
and
x\leftarrow D_{n}
.

Proof.

The proof follows directly from SubClaim
6.1
and inequality (
6
).
∎

We now define some helpful terms. For
i\in[0,n-1]
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{i}

•

Define
p_{z}:=\mathrm{Pr}_{x\leftarrow D_{n}}[x_{1\ldots i}=z]
, i.e. the probability that the first
i
bits of
x
sampled from
D_{n}
match
z
.

•

Define
p_{b|z}:=\mathrm{Pr}_{x\leftarrow D_{n}}[x_{i+1}=b|x_{1\ldots i}=z]
i.e. the probability that the
i+1
-th bit of
x
sampled from
D_{n}
is
b
conditioned on the first
i
bits of
x
matching
z
.

•

Define
\widetilde{p}_{b|z}:=\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z)=b]
.

Also, for all
j\in[0,n]
, define distribution
\widetilde{D}_{j}
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
as follows. For all
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{\widetilde{D}_{j}}[x]

\displaystyle:=p_{x_{1}}\cdot p_{x_{2}|x_{1}}\cdot p_{x_{3}|x_{1,2}}\ldots p_{x_{j}|x_{1\ldots j-1}}\cdot\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\ldots j}}\ldots\widetilde{p}_{x_{n}|x_{1\ldots n-1}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}\prod_{i=j}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}

First, note that
\widetilde{D}_{n}=D_{n}
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{\widetilde{D}_{n}}[x]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}p_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}

\displaystyle=p_{x}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}_{D_{n}}[x]

Also note that
\widetilde{D}_{0}={\mathcal{B}}(1^{n})
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{\widetilde{D}_{0}}[x]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}[B(1^{n})=x]

By the triangle inequality for statistical distance

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}({\mathcal{B}}(1^{n}),D_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SD}(\widetilde{D}_{0},\widetilde{D}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\mathsf{SD}(\widetilde{D}_{j+1},\widetilde{D}_{j})

The statistical distance between
\widetilde{D}_{j+1}
and
\widetilde{D}_{j}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}(\widetilde{D}_{j+1},\widetilde{D}_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{x}\left|\mathrm{Pr}_{\widetilde{D}_{j+1}}[x]-\mathrm{Pr}_{\widetilde{D}_{j}}[x]\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{x}\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}\left|p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\dots j}}\right|\prod_{i=j}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{x_{1\ldots j+1}}\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}\left|p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\dots j}}\right|\cdot\sum_{x_{j+2\ldots n}}\prod_{i=j}^{n-1}\widetilde{p}_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{x_{1\ldots j+1}}\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}\left|p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\dots j}}\right|

For any
x
, expanding
\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}p_{x_{i+1}|x_{1\dots i}}
shows that the expression equals
p_{x_{1\ldots j}}
, i.e. the probability of obtaining first
j
bits
x_{1\ldots j}
when sampling from
D_{n}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}(\widetilde{D}_{j+1},\widetilde{D}_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{x_{1\ldots j+1}}p_{x_{1\dots j}}\left|p_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\dots j}}-\widetilde{p}_{x_{j+1}|x_{1\dots j}}\right|

Now the right hand side of the equation equals
\mathsf{SD}(\{x_{1\ldots j},x_{j+1}\},\{x_{1\ldots j},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x_{1\ldots j})\})
when
x\leftarrow D_{n}
, which gives the following upper bound for
\mathsf{SD}({\mathcal{B}}(1^{n}),D_{n})
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}({\mathcal{B}}(1^{n}),D_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j}\mathsf{SD}(\{x_{1\ldots j},x_{j+1}\},\{x_{1\ldots j},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x_{1\ldots j})\})

where
x\leftarrow D_{n}
. If we also consider
j\leftarrow[0,n-1]
then

\displaystyle\mathsf{SD}({\mathcal{B}}(1^{n}),D_{n})

\displaystyle\leq n\cdot\mathsf{SD}(\{x_{1\ldots j},x_{j+1}\},\{x_{1\ldots j},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x_{1\ldots j})\})

\displaystyle<n\cdot(1/p(n)+1/2^{n})

\displaystyle<2n/p(n)

where the second step follows from SubClaim
6.2
and the last step holds for large enough
n
. Since
p(n)\geq 2nq(n)
, this implies

\mathsf{SD}({\mathcal{B}}(1^{n}),D_{n})<1/q(n)

which concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Claim 6.2

(Pseudo-determinism)
.

\mathrm{Pr}_{R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}}[\exists y\text{ s.t. }\mathrm{Pr}\left[{\mathcal{B}}(1^{n};R_{1},\ldots,R_{n})\neq y\right]\leq 1/2^{n}]>1-1/q(n)

Proof.

{\mathcal{B}}(1^{n};R_{1},\ldots,R_{n})
consists of loop where in the
i
-th iteration
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is run with randomness
R_{i}
. The input of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
in the
i
-th iteration (apart from the random coins
R_{i}
) is completely determined by the output of previous iterations. It therefore suffices to show that for each iteration
i
, for most strings
R_{i}
, the output of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is pseudo-deterministic , i.e. for the
i
-th iteration there exists an output
y
such that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
with random coins
R_{i}
outputs
y
with high probability.

SubClaim 6.3
.

For any
i\in[0,n-1]
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{i}
,
R\in[2^{n}]
, we say that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z;R)
has determinism error atmost
\epsilon
if there exists
y
such that

\mathrm{Pr}[{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z;R)=y]\geq 1-\epsilon

Then for all
i\in[0,n-1]
, for all
z\in\{0,1\}^{i}
,

\mathrm{Pr}_{R\leftarrow[2^{n}]}[{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z;R)\text{ has determinism error atmost }(1/n2^{n})]>1-1/nq(n)

Proof.

{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z;R)
uses
E(z)
to obtain a probability estimate
e
, and then uses the random coins
R
to output
1
if
2^{n}\cdot e>R
, else output
0
. Let
\pi
be the probability that
A(z)=1
.
E(z)
is the average of
p(n)
independent random variables that are
1
with probability
\pi
and are
0
otherwise. Therefore by setting
\delta=n
in the additive Chernoff bound (Theorem
4.1
)

\Prr_{E}\left[\left|\pi-E(z)\right|\geq 1/5nq(n)\right]\leq 2e^{-n^{2}}

Suppose
|2^{n}\cdot\pi-R|\geq 2/5nq(n)
. Then with probability atleast
2e^{-n^{2}}
,
2^{n}\cdot E(z)
and
2^{n}\cdot\pi
are on the same side of
R
, i.e. if
R<2^{n}\cdot\pi
then
R<2^{n}\cdot E(z)
with probability atleast
2e^{-n^{2}}
, while if
R>2^{n}\cdot\pi
then
R>2^{n}\cdot E(z)
with probability atleast
2e^{-n^{2}}
. Since the output of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z;R)
is entirely determined by whether or not
R<2^{n}\cdot E(z)
,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z;R)
therefore has determinism error atmost
2e^{-n^{2}}
which is less that
(1/n2^{n})
for large enough
n

All that remains to be shown is that
|2^{n}\cdot\pi-R|\geq 2/5nq(n)
holds with high enough probability. Since
R
is uniformly sampled from
[2^{n}]
, the number of values of
R
such that
|2^{n}\cdot\pi-R|<2/5nq(n)
is atmost
1+2^{n}\cdot 4/5nq(n)
. The probability that
R
is not one of these values is therefore atleast
1-4/5nq(n)-1/2^{n}
which is greater than
1-1/nq(n)
for large enough
n
.
∎

For each iteration
i
, with probability atleast
1-1/nq(n)
,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z_{i};R_{i})
has determinism error atmost
1/(n2^{n})
(where
z_{i}
is the input in the
i
-th iteration). Then

\mathrm{Pr}_{R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}}[\forall i,{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(z_{i};R_{i})\text{ has determinism error at most }1/(n2^{n})]>1-n/nq(n)=1/q(n)

The determinism error of
{\mathcal{B}}
is atmost the sum of the determinism error of its iterations, therefore

\mathrm{Pr}_{R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}}[{\mathcal{B}}(1^{n};R_{1},\ldots,R_{n})\text{ has determinism error at most }1/2^{n}]>1-1/q(n)

which concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Claim
6.1
and Claim
6.2
show that
{\mathcal{B}}
contradicts the security of
{\mathcal{D}}
which concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

7
State Puzzles are Equivalent to One-Way Puzzles

We define state puzzles, which capture the hardness of synthesizing a (secret) quantum state
|\psi_{s}\rangle
corresponding to a (public) classical string
s
, and are implied by quantum money.

Definition 7.1

(State Puzzles)
.

A state puzzle is defined by a quantum polynomial-time generator
{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n})
that outputs a classical-quantum state
(s,|\psi_{s}\rangle)
such that given
s
, it is (quantum) computationally infeasible to output
\rho
that overlaps noticeably with
|\psi_{s}\rangle
.

Formally,
for every quantum polynomial-time adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
, every (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, for large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,|\psi_{s}\rangle)\leftarrow{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n})\\
\rho\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,s)\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big[\mathsf{Tr}(|\psi_{s}\rangle\langle\psi_{s}|\rho)\Big]\leq\mathsf{negl}(n)

We also define a weaker version of state puzzles, where we require that the state output by
{\mathcal{A}}
must fail to project onto
|\psi_{s}\rangle\langle\psi_{s}|
with noticeable probability.

Definition 7.2

(
\varepsilon
-Weak State Puzzles)
.

For
\varepsilon:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, a
\varepsilon
-weak state puzzle is defined by a quantum polynomial-time generator
{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n})
that outputs a classical-quantum state
(s,|\psi_{s}\rangle)
such that given
s
, it is (quantum) computationally infeasible to output
\rho
that almost completely overlaps with
|\psi_{s}\rangle

Formally,
for
every quantum polynomial-time adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
, every (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, for large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,|\psi_{s}\rangle)\leftarrow{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n})\\
\rho\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau\rangle,s)\end{subarray}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big[\mathsf{Tr}(|\psi_{s}\rangle\langle\psi_{s}|\rho)\Big]\leq 1-\varepsilon(n)

We will sometimes simply refer to weak state puzzles. This is taken to mean
1/p(n)
-weak state puzzles for some non-zero polynomial
p
.

In this section we prove the equivalence of (weak and standard) state puzzles and (distributional and standard) one-way puzzles. We first prove that the existence of weak state puzzles implies the existence of one-way puzzles.

Theorem 7.1
.

If
1/q(n)
-weak state puzzles (Definition
7.2
) exist for some non-zero polynomial
q(\cdot)
then
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzles (Definition
4.3
) exist for some non-zero polynomial
p(\cdot)
.

Since Theorem
4.3
shows that distributional one-way puzzles can be amplified to obtain (strong) one-way puzzles, the following is a corollary of Theorem
7.1
.

Corollary 7.1
.

If
1/q(n)
-weak state puzzles (Definition
7.2
) exist for some non-zero polynomial
q(\cdot)
then one-way puzzles (Definition
4.2
) exist.

Proof.

(of Theorem
7.1
)
For some polynomial
q(\cdot)
, let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a
1/q(n)
-weak state puzzle (Definition
7.2
) that outputs
s,|\$_{s}\rangle
, where
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
|\$_{s}\rangle\in\{{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\}^{\otimes n}
. Define the algorithm
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
as follows:

1.

Sample
\mathsf{c}\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}
where
{\mathcal{C}}
is the Clifford group for
n
qubits.

2.

Sample
\mathsf{s},|\$_{\mathsf{s}}\rangle\leftarrow{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n})

3.

Compute
|\$_{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{c}}\rangle:=\mathsf{c}|\$_{\mathsf{s}}\rangle

4.

Sample
b_{\text{mode}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}

5.

If
b_{\text{mode}}=0:

(a)

Sample
i\leftarrow[0,n-1]

(b)

Measure the first
i
bits of
|\$_{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{c}}\rangle
in the computational basis to obtain measurement output
x
and residual state
|\$_{x}\rangle
. If
i=0
then
x
is the empty string and
|\$_{x}\rangle:=|\$_{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{c}}\rangle

(c)

Measure the
i+1
-th bit of
|\$_{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{c}}\rangle
(i.e. the first bit of
|\$_{x}\rangle
) to obtain measurement output
\beta
.

(d)

Let
\pi=(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{c},b_{\text{mode}},i,x)
. Output puzzle
\pi
and key
\beta
.

6.

If
b_{\text{mode}}=1:

(a)

Sample
r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{0^{n}\}

(b)

For
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, define
f_{r}(z):=\mathsf{min}(z,z\oplus r)
.

(c)

Apply
\sum_{z}|z\rangle\langle z|\otimes X^{f_{r}(z)}
to
|\$_{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{c}}\rangle|0\rangle

(d)

Measure the second register in computational basis to obtain measurement outcome
x_{0}
, and set
x_{1}=x_{0}\oplus r
. Let the residual state on the first register be
|\psi_{\text{post}}\rangle
.

(e)

Sample
b_{\text{order}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

(f)

y_{0}:=x_{b_{\text{order}}}
and
y_{1}:=x_{1-b_{\text{order}}}

(g)

Define
V_{y_{0},y_{1},b}

12
12

12

We can implement
V_{y_{0},y_{1},0}
as follows. Let
U_{0}
be a unitary that maps
|0\rangle
to
|\varphi_{0}\rangle:=\frac{|y_{0}\rangle+|y_{1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}
and let
U_{1}
be a unitary that maps
|0\rangle
to
|\varphi_{1}\rangle:=\frac{|y_{0}\rangle-|y_{1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}
. Given a state
|y_{b}\rangle|0\rangle
, apply
U_{b}
to the second register to get
|y_{b}\rangle|\varphi_{b}\rangle
. Then apply
U_{0}^{\dagger}
to the second register. If
b=0
this results in
|y_{0}\rangle|0\rangle
, else this results in
|y_{1}\rangle|\varphi\rangle
where
|\varphi\rangle=U_{0}^{\dagger}|\varphi_{1}\rangle
is some state orthogonal to
|0\rangle
. Then coherently perform the operation that applies
X^{y_{0}}
to the first register if the second register is
|0\rangle
and applies
X^{y_{1}}
to the first register if the second register is any other computational basis state. This results in
|0\rangle|0\rangle
if
b=0
and
|0\rangle|\varphi\rangle
otherwise. Finally, apply
U_{0}
to the second register to obtain
|0\rangle|\varphi_{b}\rangle
and output the second register.
V_{y_{0},y_{1},1}
can be implemented similarly.

as a unitary that maps

•

|y_{0}\rangle\mapsto\frac{|y_{0}\rangle+i^{b}|y_{1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}

•

|y_{1}\rangle\mapsto\frac{|y_{0}\rangle-i^{b}|y_{1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}

(h)

Sample
b_{\text{rot}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

(i)

Apply
V_{y_{0},y_{1},b_{\text{rot}}}
to
|\psi_{\text{post}}\rangle
and measure in computational basis to obtain outcome
y
.

(j)

If
y=y_{0}
then
\beta\leftarrow 0
, else
\beta\leftarrow 1
.

(k)

Let
\pi=(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{c},b_{\text{mode}},y_{0},y_{1},b_{\text{rot}})
. Output puzzle
\pi
and key
\beta
.

Let
k
be a constant greater than
6
such that for large enough
n
,
n^{k}\geq q(n)^{3}
and let
p(\cdot)
be a polynomial such that
p(n)\geq n^{64k}
.
We will prove that
\mathsf{Samp}
is a
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzle. Note that this suffices to prove Theorem
7.1
.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that
\mathsf{Samp}
is
not
a
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzle. By the definition of
1/p(n)
-distributional one-way puzzle, there exists a QPT
{\mathcal{A}}=\{A_{n}\}_{{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and (non-uniform, quantum) advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle=\{|\tau_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for infinitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\{\pi,\beta\}_{(\pi,\beta)\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}\approx_{1/p(n)}\{\pi,{\mathcal{A}}(|\tau_{\mathsf{}}\rangle,\pi)\}_{(\pi,\beta)\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}

Fix any such adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
, any such advice ensemble
|\tau\rangle
, and any such large enough
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
We will use this adversary to build a reduction that contradicts the security of
{\mathcal{G}}
. For all
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
c\in{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
, define
\Delta_{s,c}
as follows:

•

Let
D_{0}
be the distribution of
(\pi,\beta)
when
\pi,\beta
is sampled by
\mathsf{Samp}
conditioned on
\mathsf{s}=s
and
\mathsf{c}=c
.

•

Let
D_{1}
be the distribution of
(\pi,A(\pi,|\tau_{\mathsf{}}\rangle))
when
\pi,\beta
is sampled by
\mathsf{Samp}
conditioned on
\mathsf{s}=s
and
\mathsf{c}=c
.

•

\Delta_{s,c}:=SD(D_{0},D_{1})

Intuitively,
\Delta_{s,c}
is the adversary’s error in sampling from the true distribution when
\mathsf{s}=s
and
\mathsf{c}=c
.
We will use the adversary to synthesize an approximation of
|\$_{s,c}\rangle
for a random choice of
s,c
, following the pattern of Aaronson’s synthesis algorithm. The algorithm queries a PP oracle to obtain the values of probabilities and phases. The reduction cannot query a PP oracle, so we will replace the query responses with estimates obtained by querying the adversary.

First, we perform the real amplitude step of Aaronson synthesis.

Claim 7.1

(Real Amplitude Synthesis)
.

Let
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
c\in{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
. Let
|\tau_{\mathsf{amp}}\rangle:=|0\rangle|\tau_{\mathsf{}}\rangle^{\otimes np(n)}
. Then there exists an efficient unitary
\widetilde{M}_{s,c}
such that

\left|\widetilde{M}_{s,c}|0\rangle|\tau_{\mathsf{amp}}\rangle-|\$^{*}_{s,c}\rangle|\tau_{\mathsf{amp}}\rangle\right|\leq\sqrt{3n^{3}}/p(n)^{1/4}+2\sqrt{n\Delta_{s,c}}

Additionally, there is a uniform circuit family that takes
(s,c)
as input and implements
\widetilde{M}_{s,c}
.

Proof.

Fix any
s
and
c
. We first define some terms that will be useful for the proof.

•

Interpret
|\$_{s,c}\rangle
as
\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{z}e^{-i\phi_{z}}|z\rangle
where
a_{z}\geq 0
and
\phi_{z}\in[0,2\pi)
.

•

|\$^{*}_{s,c}\rangle:=\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{z}|z\rangle
. Intuitively,
|\$_{s,c}^{*}\rangle
represents
|\$_{s,c}\rangle
with the phase information removed, i.e., with real amplitudes.

•

For all
i\in[1,n]
, for all
z\in\{0,1\}^{i}
,

–

p_{z}:=\Prr[x=z|b_{\text{mode}}=0\wedge i=i^{\prime}]
represents the probability that the first
i
bits equal
z
when measuring
|\$_{s,c}\rangle
in the computational basis.

•

For all
i\in[0,n-1]
, for all
z\in\{0,1\}^{i}
, for all
b\in\{0,1\}
,

–

p_{b|z}:=\Prr[\beta=b|x=z\wedge b_{\text{mode}}=0\wedge i=i^{\prime}]

represents the probability that the
i+1
th bit equals
b
conditioned on the first
i
bits equalling
z
when measuring
|\$_{s,c}\rangle
in the computational basis.

–

\widetilde{p}_{b|z}:=\Prr[b={\mathcal{A}}(s,c,0,i,z,|\tau_{\mathsf{}}\rangle)]

We first use
A
to obtain a good estimate for
p_{1|z}
by defining an estimator
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(z)
.
For all
i\in[0,n-1]
, for all
z\in\{0,1\}^{i}
define
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(z)
that takes advice
|\tau_{\mathsf{}}\rangle^{\otimes p(n)}
as follows:

•

For
j=1
to
p(n)
:

–

B_{j}\leftarrow A(s,c,0,i,z,|\tau_{\mathsf{}}\rangle)

•

Return
\sum_{j}B_{j}/p(n)

The rest of the construction of
\widetilde{M}_{s,c}
proceeds as in the real amplitude step of Aaronson synthesis, except with the oracle queries replaced with estimates given by
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\cdot)
.

Superposition queries to
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\cdot)
may produce entangled junk so we must later on uncompute to remove the junk. To do so we define
E_{i}
to be the purification of
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
that acts on input register
\mathsf{Z}
, output register
\mathsf{V}
, and advice register
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}
, i.e.,

E_{i}|z\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}}|0\rangle_{\mathsf{V}}|\tau_{\mathsf{1}}\rangle_{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}=|z\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}}\sum_{v}\sqrt{\Prr[v={\mathcal{E}}_{i}(z)]}|v\rangle_{\mathsf{V}}|\mathsf{junk}_{v}\rangle_{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}

where
|\tau_{\mathsf{1}}\rangle:=|\tau_{\mathsf{}}\rangle^{\otimes p(n)}|0\rangle
and
|\mathsf{junk}_{v}\rangle
is some normalized state.

For all
v\geq 0
, define
|\psi_{v}\rangle:=\sqrt{v}|1\rangle+\sqrt{1-v}|0\rangle
and let
P
be a unitary that maps
|v\rangle_{\mathsf{V}}|0\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}}
to
|v\rangle_{\mathsf{V}}|\psi_{v}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}}
13
13

13

We may only be able to efficiently implement
P
upto some exponentially small error, however, this small error will not affect our result so we will elide it for the sake of clarity.
. Let
\mathsf{X}=\{\mathsf{X}_{i}\}_{i\in[1,n]},\mathsf{A}=\{\mathsf{A}_{i}\}_{i\in[1,n]},\mathsf{A}^{\prime}=\{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[1,n]}
be collections of registers where each
\mathsf{X}_{i}
is a single qubit. Define
\widetilde{M}_{i}
that acts on
\mathsf{X}_{1\ldots i+1},\mathsf{A}_{i},
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}_{i}
as follows:

•

Let
\mathsf{Z}:=\mathsf{X}_{1\ldots i}
,
\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{X}_{i+1},\mathsf{V}:=\mathsf{A}_{i},\mathsf{V}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{A}^{\prime}_{i}

•

Apply
\left(E_{i}\right)^{\dagger}PE_{i}
to
\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{Z}^{\prime}\mathsf{V}\mathsf{V}

Define
