\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{C}\left[C^{\otimes 2}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|^{\otimes 2}C^{\dagger\otimes 2}\right]=\frac{{\mathbb{I}}+{\mathbb{F}}}{2^{n}(2^{n}+1)}

We can now compute
{\mathbb{E}}_{C}[p_{C}(x)^{2}]
as follows

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{C}[p_{C}(x)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\langle x|^{\otimes 2}\frac{{\mathbb{I}}+{\mathbb{F}}}{2^{n}(2^{n}+1)}|x\rangle^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{2^{n}(2^{n}+1)}

Since the above holds for any
x
, it also holds for uniformly sampled
x
, which implies

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{x,C}[p_{C}(x)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{2^{n}(2^{n}+1)}

Substituting in
(
19
)
gives

\displaystyle\Prr_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\\
C\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}}\end{subarray}}\left[|p_{C}(x)-1/2^{n}|\geq\frac{\alpha}{2^{n}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}

We therefore obtain a bound on the fraction of
x
with
p_{C}(x)
above a threshold. However, we need to bound the total probability mass on such
x
. To do so we leverage the fact that the above bound holds for every
\alpha
. We can therefore simultaneously bound the fraction of such
x
for every a sequence of
\alpha
values. This turns out to be sufficient to bound the probability mass.

Define
f_{\alpha}(C):=\Prr_{x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}}\left[p_{C}(x)\geq\frac{1+\alpha}{2^{n}}\right]
. Intuitively, this is the fraction of
x
with
p_{C}(x)
above the required threshold.
Therefore

{\mathbb{E}}_{C}[f_{\alpha}(C)]\leq 1/\alpha^{2}

By a Markov argument

\Prr_{C}[f_{\alpha}(C)\geq 1/\alpha^{1.5}]\leq 3/\alpha^{0.5}

For any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
, setting
\alpha
to
n^{2i}

\Prr_{C}[f_{n^{2i}}(C)\geq 1/n^{3i}]\leq 3/n^{i}

Let
{\mathbb{G}}_{i}:=\left\{C:f_{n^{2i}}(C)<1/n^{3i}\right\}
. Then
\Prr_{C}[C\in{\mathbb{G}}_{i}]\geq 1-3/n^{i}
. Let
{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}:=\bigcap_{i\geq k+3}{\mathbb{G}}_{i}
. Then

\displaystyle\Prr_{C}[C\in{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\geq 1-\left(3/n^{k+3}+3/n^{k+4}+\ldots\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{3}{n^{k+3}}(1+1/n+1/n^{2}+\ldots)

\displaystyle\geq 1-6/n^{k+3}

Fix any
C\in{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\sum_{x:p_{C}(x)\geq\frac{1+n^{2k+6}}{2^{n}}}p_{C}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq k+3}\sum_{x:\frac{1+n^{2i+2}}{2^{n}}\geq p_{C}(x)\geq\frac{1+n^{2i}}{2^{n}}}p_{C}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\geq k+3}\sum_{x:\frac{1+n^{2i+2}}{2^{n}}\geq p_{C}(x)\geq\frac{1+n^{2i}}{2^{n}}}\frac{1+n^{2i+2}}{2^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\geq k+3}\frac{2^{n}}{n^{3i}}\cdot\frac{1+n^{2i+2}}{2^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\geq k+3}\frac{2^{n}}{n^{3i}}\cdot\frac{1+n^{2i+2}}{2^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\geq k+3}2/n^{i-2}

\displaystyle\leq 4/n^{k+1}

where the third step uses the definitions of
{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
,
{\mathbb{G}}_{i}
, and
f_{n^{2i}}
. For
C\in{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
, all
k>6
and sufficiently large
n

\sum_{x:p_{C}(x)\geq\frac{n^{3k}}{2^{n}}}p_{C}(x)\leq 1/n^{k}

and
\Prr_{C}[C\in{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}]\geq 1-1/n^{k}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
7.3

Theorem
7.3
.

Let
(x,y),(x^{*},y^{*})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that
\exists\gamma>0,\gamma^{\prime}>0
s.t.

•

x^{2}+y^{2}\geq\gamma^{2}

•

(x-x^{*})^{2}+(y-y^{*})^{2}\leq(\gamma^{\prime})^{2}

•

\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma

Then
|e^{-i\cdot\mathsf{arctan2}(y,x)}-e^{-i\cdot\mathsf{arctan2}(y^{*},x^{*})}|\leq 2\gamma^{\prime}/\gamma

Proof.

We prove this theorem by a geometric argument. Let
X=(x,y)
and
Y=(x^{*},y^{*})
be points on the Cartesian plane, and let
O=(0,0)
be the origin. Let the (smaller) angle between rays
OX
and
OY
be
\zeta
. Then
|\mathsf{arctan2}(y,x)-\mathsf{arctan2}(y^{*},x^{*})|
is equal to
\zeta
(upto a multiple of
2\pi
offset). By expanding the expression we aim to bound

\displaystyle|e^{-i\cdot\mathsf{arctan2}(y,x)}-e^{-i\cdot\mathsf{arctan2}(y^{*},x^{*})}|

\displaystyle=\left|2\sin\left(\frac{\mathsf{arctan2}(y,x)-\mathsf{arctan2}(y^{*},x^{*})}{2}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|2\sin\left(\frac{\zeta}{2}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq|\zeta|

Therefore it suffices to show that
\zeta
is upper bounded by
2\gamma^{\prime}/\gamma
.

Let
C
be a circle of radius
\gamma^{\prime}
centered at
X
. Since
|OX|=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}\geq\gamma>\gamma^{\prime}
,
O
is strictly external to the circle. Additionally, since
|XY|=\sqrt{(x-x^{*})^{2}+(y-y^{*})^{2}}\leq\gamma^{\prime}
,
Y
is on the circle or internal to it. Therefore,
OY
is either a tangent or secant line. The angle between
OX
and
OY
is maximized if
OY
is tangent to the circle, in which case
OY\bot XY
and
\sin(\zeta)=|XY|/|OX|\leq\gamma^{\prime}/\gamma
. Additionally note that
\zeta
is acute, so
\zeta\leq 2\sin(\zeta)\leq 2\gamma^{\prime}/\gamma
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Quantum Advantage from One-Way Puzzles in Microcrypt

In this section we show that if one-way functions
do not
exist, the existence of one-way puzzles implies sampling based quantum advantage
17
17

17

The first version of this manuscript had the informal argument sketched out in the Results section, but we have added a formal proof to the current (updated) version.
. Let
(\mathsf{Samp},\mathsf{Ver})
be a one-way puzzle. Then we claim that the existence of an efficient classical algorithm that samples from a distribution less than
1/3
statistically-far from the distribution of
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
on all large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
implies the existence of one-way functions.

Suppose there exists an efficient classical algorithm
{\mathcal{S}}
such that for all large enough
n

\mathsf{SD}(\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n}),{\mathcal{S}}(1^{n};r))\leq 1/3

where
r
is a uniform string of
n
-bits.

Claim D.1
.

Define
f(x)
for
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
as follows:

•

(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})\leftarrow{\mathcal{S}}(1^{n};x)

•

Output
s^{\prime}

Then
f
is a
1/n
-distributional one-way function.

Proof.

Suppose not, i.e. there exists a non-uniform PPT adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
such that for infinitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

\{s^{\prime},x\}\approx_{1/n}\{s^{\prime},{\mathcal{A}}(s^{\prime})\}

(20)

where
x
is a uniform
n
-bit string and
(s^{\prime},k^{\prime}):={\mathcal{S}}(1^{n};x)
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
be the algorithm that on input
x
performs the following:

•

s^{*},k^{*}\leftarrow{\mathcal{S}}(1^{n};x)

•

Return
k^{*}

Consider the distribution
\{s^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{A}}(s^{\prime}))\}
. Since
\{s^{\prime},{\mathcal{A}}(s^{\prime})\}
is at most
1/n
far from
\{s^{\prime},x\}
,
\{s^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{A}}(s^{\prime}))\}
is also (at most)
1/n
far from
\{s^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(x)\}
. Since
(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})
is obtained by running
{\mathcal{S}}(1^{n};x)
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(x)
simply outputs
k^{\prime}
,
\{s^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(x)\}
is identical to
\{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\}
. This gives

\{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\}\approx_{1/n}\{s^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{A}}(s^{\prime}))\}

Since the distribution over
(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})
is
1/3
close to the distribution of
\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})

\mathsf{SD}\left(\{s,k\},\{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}\}\right)\leq 1/3\text{ and }\mathsf{SD}\left(\{s,{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(s))\},\{s^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{A}}(s^{\prime}))\}\right)\leq 1/3

where
(s,k)\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})
. Putting these together with the previous equation we get

\mathsf{SD}\left(\{s,k\},\{s,{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(s))\}\right)\leq 2/3+1/n

Now, by the correctness of the one-way puzzle

\Prr_{s,k\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[\mathsf{Ver}(s,k)=1]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(n)

which implies

\Prr_{s,k\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp}(1^{n})}[\mathsf{Ver}(s,{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)))=1]\geq 1/3-1/n-\mathsf{negl}(n)

which contradicts the security of the one-way puzzle.
∎

A Note on Quantum Advantage from One-way Puzzles.

Since the existence of distributional one-way functions implies the existence of one-way functions
[
ILL89
]
, if one-way puzzles exist but one-way functions do not, there exist distributions that can be efficiently sampled by quantum machines but cannot be efficiently sampled by classical machines.
It was shown in
[
MY24
]

that the existence of one-way functions implies (interactive, inefficiently verifiable) proofs of quantumness. Combining the previous two facts yields a direct proof that the existence of one-way puzzles implies interactive quantum advantage.
